=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Given two integers, n and k, an alternating permutation is a permutation of the first n positive integers such that no two adjacent elements are both odd or both even.
Return the k-th alternating permutation sorted in lexicographical order. If there are fewer than k valid alternating permutations, return an empty list.
 
Example 1:

Input: n = 4, k = 6
Output: [3,4,1,2]
Explanation:
The lexicographically-sorted alternating permutations of [1, 2, 3, 4] are:

[1, 2, 3, 4]
[1, 4, 3, 2]
[2, 1, 4, 3]
[2, 3, 4, 1]
[3, 2, 1, 4]
[3, 4, 1, 2] ← 6th permutation
[4, 1, 2, 3]
[4, 3, 2, 1]

Since k = 6, we return [3, 4, 1, 2].

Example 2:

Input: n = 3, k = 2
Output: [3,2,1]
Explanation:
The lexicographically-sorted alternating permutations of [1, 2, 3] are:

[1, 2, 3]
[3, 2, 1] ← 2nd permutation

Since k = 2, we return [3, 2, 1].

Example 3:

Input: n = 2, k = 3
Output: []
Explanation:
The lexicographically-sorted alternating permutations of [1, 2] are:

[1, 2]
[2, 1]

There are only 2 alternating permutations, but k = 3, which is out of range. Thus, we return an empty list [].

 
Constraints:

1 <= n <= 100
1 <= k <= 10^15

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def permute(self, n: int, k: int) -> List[int]:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Pseudo:

from typing import List
class Solution:
    def permute(self, n: int, k: int) -> List[int]:
        fact = [1]*(n+1)
        for i in range(1,n+1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1]*i

        def count_ways(odd, even, first_parity):
            # first_parity: 0 for even? Need choose representation.
            # Let's use parity = num % 2: 1 odd, 0 even.
            if odd < 0 or even < 0:
                return 0
            if first_parity == 1: # odd
                if odd == even or odd == even + 1:
                    return fact[odd] * fact[even]
                return 0
            else: # even
                if even == odd or even == odd + 1:
                    return fact[odd] * fact[even]
                return 0

        used = [False]*(n+1)
        res = []
        odd_rem = (n+1)//2
        even_rem = n//2
        prev_parity = -1  # no previous

        for pos in range(n):
            chosen = None
            # Determine allowed parity: if pos==0, any? Actually we can iterate all and count_ways will reject impossible starts. But for efficiency, allowed = -1 if no prev else 1-prev.
            # If prev_parity != -1, only opposite parity can be chosen. But if we iterate all and skip used/parity mismatch, okay.
            for x in range(1, n+1):
                if used[x]:
                    continue
                p = x % 2
                if prev_parity != -1 and p == prev_parity:
                    continue
                # remove x from remaining counts
                if p == 1:
                    new_odd = odd_rem - 1
                    new_even = even_rem
                else:
                    new_odd = odd_rem
                    new_even = even_rem - 1
                # after placing x, next parity must be opposite
                next_parity = 1 - p
                cnt = count_ways(new_odd, new_even, next_parity)
                if k > cnt:
                    k -= cnt
                else:
                    chosen = x
                    break
            if chosen is None:
                return []
            res.append(chosen)
            used[chosen] = True
            if chosen % 2 == 1:
                odd_rem -= 1
            else:
                even_rem -= 1
            prev_parity = chosen % 2
        return res

Let's draft code:

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def permute(self, n: int, k: int) -> List[int]:
        fact = [1] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i

        def ways(odd: int, even: int, first_parity: int) -> int:
            # first_parity: 1 = odd, 0 = even
            if odd < 0 or even < 0:
                return 0
            if first_parity == 1:
                if odd == even or odd == even + 1:
                    return fact[odd] * fact[even]
                return 0
            else:
                if even == odd or even == odd + 1:
                    return fact[odd] * fact[even]
                return 0

        used = [False] * (n + 1)
        ans = []
        odd_rem = (n + 1) // 2
        even_rem = n // 2
        prev_parity = -1

        for _ in range(n):
            chosen = -1
            for x in range(1, n + 1):
                if used[x]:
                    continue
                p = x & 1
                if prev_parity != -1 and p == prev_parity:
                    continue

                if p == 1:
                    no, ne = odd_rem - 1, even_rem
                else:
                    no, ne = odd_rem, even_rem - 1

                cnt = ways(no, ne, 1 - p)
                if k > cnt:
                    k -= cnt
                else:
                    chosen = x
                    break

            if chosen == -1:
                return []

            ans.append(chosen)
            used[chosen] = True
            if chosen & 1:
                odd_rem -= 1
            else:
                even_rem -= 1
            prev_parity = chosen & 1

        return ans

Need check `ways(no, ne, 1 - p)` where p is 0/1. If p=1, next parity 0 even; if p=0, next parity 1 odd. Good.

This means if next element must be odd but no odd remaining and one even remaining, impossible. Correct. Suppose odd=1, even=0 -> true (1==0 false, 1==1 true) count 1. Correct. For first_parity even: even==odd or even==odd+1. Suppose odd=0, even=1 -> true count1. Correct. Suppose odd=1, even=0 -> false. Correct.

Need maybe if no, ne are negative? We only call after removing a candidate of that parity from remaining, so no/ne nonnegative if candidate exists. But if bug, ways handles negative.

Let's maybe prove count_ways exact:
Given remaining odd count o, even count e, and required first parity p. Any completion is an alternating sequence of length L=o+e starting with p. The parity pattern is uniquely determined: positions with parity p are ceil(L/2), other floor(L/2). To use all remaining numbers, the number of odd positions in pattern must equal o and even positions e. If p=odd, odd positions = ceil(L/2), even=floor(L/2). Conditions o=ceil(L/2), e=floor(L/2), equivalent to o==e or o==e+1. If p=even, e==o or e==o+1. If condition holds, each parity class can be arranged arbitrarily in its positions: o! e!. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
again.</think>```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def permute(self, n: int, k: int) -> List[int]:
        fact = [1] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i

        def ways(odd: int, even: int, first_parity: int) -> int:
            # first_parity: 1 = odd, 0 = even
            if odd < 0 or even < 0:
                return 0
            if first_parity == 1:
                if odd == even or odd == even + 1:
                    return fact[odd] * fact[even]
                return 0
            else:
                if even == odd or even == odd + 1:
                    return fact[odd] * fact[even]
                return 0

        used = [False] * (n + 1)
        ans = []
        odd_rem = (n + 1) // 2
        even_rem = n // 2
        prev_parity = -1

        for _ in range(n):
            chosen = -1

            for x in range(1, n + 1):
                if used[x]:
                    continue

                p = x & 1
                if prev_parity != -1 and p == prev_parity:
                    continue

                if p == 1:
                    no, ne = odd_rem - 1, even_rem
                else:
                    no, ne = odd_rem, even_rem - 1

                cnt = ways(no, ne, 1 - p)

                if k > cnt:
                    k -= cnt
                else:
                    chosen = x
                    break

            if chosen == -1:
                return []

            ans.append(chosen)
            used[chosen] = True

            if chosen & 1:
                odd_rem -= 1
            else:
                even_rem -= 1

            prev_parity = chosen & 1

        return ans
```
